Case Studies he makes three particular points about the nature of the relationship between experiment and theory. experiments have: less observations than one would expect—to establish the difference between laws and generalizations because ‘to establish the form of a regularity in physics only a few careful observations are needed.’ A difficulty in explaining how subsequent applications of a theory are related to the observations by which the theory was originally established.38 In his next observation he notes the same problem present in the map: To take the two difficulties together: it is worth noticing they arise for theories as much as, and no more than, for maps. not all the applications to which a theory is put need have been specifically made in the course of the experimental investigation by which it was established. But nor need all the things which can be read off of from a map have been specifically put in. A child might wonder how it was possible ever to produce a map at all, since to tread every inch even of a small area, and to measure all the distances and directions one can read off a map, would take an unlimited length of time. This, of course, is the marvel of cartography: the fact that, from a limited number of highly precise and well-chosen measurements and observations, one can produce a map from which can be read off an unlimited number of geographical facts of almost as great a precision.39 (emphasis mine)

This doesn’t undermine the utility of the map or the diagram in hypothesis making. only through this kind of ‘fuzzy visual math’ are ‘unlimited number of inferences of comparable accuracy’ made from a limited number of highly accurate observations.40 guidelines for working with observation are robust in cartography and the sciences. Questions about how data is acquired, evaluated and applied in the map arise often and this section addresses some of the theoretical and practical questions related to data.

Maps of course require us to interpret and interpolate meaning from visual signs standing in for characteristics or qualities. Using nehemiah grew’s maps of tree growth from The Anatomy of Plants (1680) demonstrates how (Figures 4.18 and 4.19). These maps representing the cellular structure of tree cores allow multiple interpretations of seemingly precise information. ‘Seemingly’ because the visual representation is neither precise or exact—we don’t know how large or how many cells are in a tree trunk and doubt they would look like this in reality, but from the map we infer a number of useful qualities of the cells of tree branches. In a similar vein Descartes’ map of Vortices (Figure 4.20) was a popular explanation for the movement of the planetary bodies through the ether, the dense air-like substance believed to fix bodies in space. Describing the heavens in this way worked as a theoretical philosophy, but in practice was not particularly good at explaining phenomena. In hindsight, Descartes’ vortices served an important cultural role as an underlying philosophy for a Copernican sun-centered theory, but few of the scientific ideas developed as explanations for the orbits of planetary bodies.

4.20 From René Descartes, ‘Vortices,’ from the Principia philosophiae (1644).

4.18, 4.19 The images are from Nehemiah Grew, Anatomy of Plants (1682).

Grew published a detailed study of plant anatomy credited as the beginning of modern comparative anatomy. The plant anatomy as drawn suggests comparisons with the organs and structures of human bodies.

These studies contributed to a general debate in the seventeenth century that plants did not have internal structure with distinct organs. Grew believed there were similarities of function between animals and plants leading him to look for the same structures in both.

These maps are a hypothesis of how plant bodies are like human bodies. This mechanist view was ‘a deliberate attempt to offer explanations of phenomena in corporeal, material or physical terms,’ however these difficult terms were interpreted.

Anatomy studies are atlases or maps representing physical bodies reduced often to their salient characteristics. Nehemiah Grew, Anatomy of Plants, 1682, plate LXXIX and table XXXVI.

Case Studies ‘The relationship between the map and the geographical statement,’ is, as Toulmin points out, ‘of a different, non-deductive kind.’41 one cannot understand the movement of planets from the map, but the map presents everything contained in the set of observational phenomena (whether right or wrong) in a logical manner so ‘the aggregate of discrete observations is transformed into a simple and connected picture, much as the collection of readings in a surveyor’s note-book is transformed into a clear and orderly map.’42 This is interesting when related to the observation and cataloguing of natural phenomena often made by designers deciding how to address a particular problem in context: it is the transformation of the observations to the map and the theoretical framework more than the description of the phenomena accounting for the kinds of conclusions possible to draw.

As a representational device the map supports this precisely because of the imprecision of signs—like Descartes’ vortices produce more in their misreading than clear understanding.

Quantitative and Qualitative Data Definitions of terms vary slightly but in general quantitative data can be measured using quanta or numbers and tends to approximate phenomena using analytical methods. Qualitative data is characterized as an approach to particular examples of phenomena trying to describe it in depth. research is an iterative process in which data gathering, analysis and representation do not proceed along a linear course, but are usually fluid and messy.43 knigge and Cope commenting on the idea of the iterative process note, ‘This kind of purposeful recursive data exploration is a shared characteristic of both grounded theory and spatial data exploration and visualization (emphasis mine). They advocate for a ‘grounded visualization’ encompassing four areas of commonality between grounded theory enabling rigorous qualitative analysis, and visualization: 1) both are exploratory, 2) iterative and recursive, 3) enable simultaneous consideration of particular instances and general patterns, and 4) encourage multiple views and perspective for building knowledge.44 I propose that using a grounded theory approach facilitates the multiplicity of possible data and the internal consistency of the logic of the map.

It is demonstrable that a ‘grounded theory’ approach aids research in geography and some of these methods are worth recounting.45 Using grounded research requires the researcher to be clear and open in reporting research procedures so overall findings may be judged in terms of the validity and credibility of the data, the plausibility and value of the findings, the theoretical formulations, the adequacy of the research process generated, and the empirical grounding of the research findings.46 In addition, findings need to be replicable. But while general consensus reigns regarding standards for quantitative data not applied to qualitative data, there remains an expectation of rigor and recognition of the importance of dissemination and communication of methodologies. Finally, grounded theory is deeply concerned with enabling rigorous qualitative research in which theory is held accountable to empirical data.47 one of the reasons grounded research is a useful idea to develop in the context of design studies is it ‘is an attempt to acknowledge analysis to be a social practice with all its attendant subjectivities, partial knowledge, and positionalities, but it is one seeking to find rigorous,

Case Studies verifiable, and explicit ways to draw conclusions.’48 knigge and Cope conclude a grounded visualization approach: offers a way to combine qualitative and quantitative data together with a ‘commitment’ to iterative, reflexive rounds of analysis, enables research to be attuned to multiple subjectivities, truths, and meanings. By building on areas of commonality between grounded theory and visualization—being exploratory, iterative—recursive, considering both the particular and the general, and allowing multiple versions of reality to emerge—grounded visualization carries great potential for building theory that is both ‘grounded’ in everyday life and real experiences, and ‘situated’ by context and broader trends and patterns.

grounded visualization is a particularly geographical set of analyses that could be used broadly thereby expanding the relevance and capabilities of spatial perspectives to other researchers. Specifically, grounded visualization is sensitive to scale issues, from local to global and back again; integrates mobility and flows over time and space; greatly depends on both qualitative and quantitative measures of context (historical and geographical); and, finally (as shown in our case studies), enables rich explorations of place.49 This approach coincides with similar problems faced in design research given the blurry line between quantitative and qualitative data. Additionally, scalar data occurs synchronically and diachronically. It can be subjective and depends on historical, social, or geographical contexts. A plethora of recent concerns about the types of data used in the map concern the way data is gathered and the formats used to represent it beginning with the introduction of geospatial information systems (gIS). gIS data divides into two formats: raster and vector. It is worth briefly comparing these differences to ‘look under the hood’ of the gIS machinery.

how gIS data is classified and organized unmasks general questions regarding data in maps.

raster files are a matrix of digital cells: each cell contains a value representing quantitative information—information with a quanta or numerical value on a scale like temperature, land use/cover, elevation, depth, or intensity of vegetation. Vector data consists of points, lines, and polygons—again digital—representing location, distance or area of landscape features. Although one would think of vector data as quantitative, it is usually generated from gIS processes through a qualitative approach, whereas raster data linked to the number of pixels on a screen tends to be gathered using sophisticated remote sensing techniques that are quantative.50 Most vector data is generated using high- resolution aerial photos or satellite images requiring processing and screen digitizing by hand. human interpretation of how this data is catalogued and validated is part of why it is considered qualitative not quantitative. A good example of this is land-use data generated for the purposes of municipal maps. Categories or zones are assigned, e.g. commercial, housing, institutional, where the blanket assessment of an area zoned ‘housing’ may not be true. Within a given area are likely people with home offices, garage laboratories, or possibly houses used solely for production of illegal drugs. The assigned category describes ‘multiple representation’ and refers to the various methods for symbolizing data. But as Slocum points out regarding the map:

Case Studies The postmodernist should be interested in this approach because it concurs with the notion there is no single `correct’ way of visualizing data. Additionally, however, the postmodernist would be interested in the multiple meanings and potentially hidden agendas found in a particular thematic map.’51 A similar example is the Map of U.S Data Network and Cost of Energy per kWh to Mean Temperature in February map (Figure 4.21). The map indexes five conditions: 1) average cost of energy per kWh by state, 2) zone temperatures, 3) location of data cable hubs and transmission lines, 4) network ownership, and 5) location of railway lines. The comparison is a gap-analysis identifying opportunities for the strategic expansion of the data network using the relationship between current hubs, temperature and cost of electricity. The ideology of the map may not be overtly polemical—it is an argument about the relationship between a visible resource (electricity) and its invisible demand made by server farms powering the Internet. The map identifies locations for server farms in the U.S. balancing cost of power, outside temperature, and access to existing network cable to supply using a sustainability model. The qualitative selection of data points reveals that contrary to current locations for major network hubs in states with warm climates or coastal locations where temperatures are milder, states with cooler climates and less expensive energy are optimal for reducing cost. however, given their smaller populations and weaker political representation these states aren’t hubs. If anything the map demonstrates in the politics of power, literally and figuratively, numbers matter.

Case Studies These case studies are from my mapping course given at Washington University in St. louis, harvard University, and Florida International University. Student maps range broadly between quantitative and qualitative analysis and include thematic, choreographic, ichnographic and topographic maps to name a few. The following selections illustrate the capacity of the map through measurement, or better, filtering the world (however relative) to act as a document of observations—in short, a kind of ‘scientized document.’ Mark Monmonier famously showed not only do all maps lie, they must.52 The lie Monmonier refers to is not one of commission, but omission. As documents of observations, maps are by definition reduced models of reality reflecting the mapmaker’s dialogue with the world. The course taught students how to hypothesize and communicate their ideas to themselves effectively through maps. exercises and readings focused on the necessary grammar and syntax of mapmaking, but the methodology required students to unpack, repack and then generate maps. If theory was the instigator of thought, application was the handmaid to facilitate a deeper understanding of the mechanics of thinking cartographically. emphasis wasn’t placed on technologies used in making maps (gIS, mathematical projection, CAD programs), but rather on maps as representational strategies. Students were encouraged to use whatever technology appropriate to the subject of their map including video, digital software, hand drawings, gIS, and physical and digital modeling. required were two mapping projects and a series of short exercises. The ﬁrst project was an unmapping problem in which students choose one of ﬁve pre-selected maps, deconstruct them to understand the map’s organization, then reassemble components of the original map into a new map according

Richter creates a rhythm with the elements conveying movement through space. His films are conscious mapping of a sequence of movements using a limited and controlled vocabulary of visual elements. The final map used a dataset selected by the student. restrictions weren’t placed on the choice of data or how it was organized, but the ﬁnal representation needed to conform to the terms and functions outlined in the mapmaking methodology. The method wasn’t limited to phenomenal problems, but applied also to ideas or concepts. It asks that students calibrate their understanding by translating from one idea or condition to another. Depending on the subject of the map, data was quantitative based on numerical measurement or qualitative highlighting a correspondence between qualia or the subjective properties of experience.53 Case Study 1: Orthographic Projections and the Grid Map: Canopy_Florida_11.09.09, 10:00AM Sara Johnson’s Canopy_Florida (Figure 4.25) is a thematic map looking at density of foliage.

The map frames a narrow slice of Floridian understory and calibrates luminosity. The goal in the map was to evaluate an existing natural condition formulating an artiﬁcial response through a shading device. Johnson’s observation of the site related to the quality of filtered light, but she wasn’t certain at first how to measure this condition. look for patterns? look for density of material? Use a light meter? When and for what duration? her dataset was a series of photographs taken during a site visit. This determined the scope of problems she could study. After consideration of what was at hand—a series of images taken during a specific time of day—Johnson opted to analyze the specific conditions of light penetration through the tree canopy. The frame of her map is limited to data taken at 10:00AM on a given site in the Florida everglades.

The map outlines the sequence of translation from original canopy condition to reformulated density. Using a typical condition Johnson projected a rationalized grid to 1) fix the distribution of light and dark as patterns in the scene, 2) visualize the general pattern of light and dark abstracted from the leaf cover, and 3) create a flexible framework for measuring the differences from one quadrant to another in the field of light and dark (Figure 4.26). Imposing a grid is a common tactic in mapping to analyze relative relationships and is distinct from a map projection involving translation between scales.

Scale is of little value in this map as Johnson treats the photograph as a topology or surface preserving its properties under continuous deformations similar to a subway map where it is impossible to understand a measured distance between the stops of the train. The train stops relative relationships in the map remain the same whether you distort the map (by folding say) or stretching it. By translating the original photograph into a high-contrast image and mapping the data on the grid, she allowed for the construction of a new value- set. This was reprojected onto the grid using a set of predetermined parameters to inform an alternative reading of the relative density of light in the original condition. Johnson tested an alternative reading of the density as a color mapping. Color and change in tonal value is characteristic of luminosity in human vision. Finally, the z-axis translation is projected as a three-dimensional lattice structure in the ﬁnal translation of the original perceived densities.

The ﬁnal mapping informed the design of an artiﬁcial canopy intended to mitigate or augment the natural canopy of the Florida site. By looking carefully and analytically at the

Case Studies condition of luminosity in the test-bed, Johnson interpolated the parameters for the new design. The mapping is effective because she was speciﬁc about the data and translations.

Similar to sonar maps translating one dataset (sound) to another (graphical index) to describe a third condition (terrain), this map translates an original image made using light (photo) to a density reading and ﬁnally a three-dimensional representation of a new perceptual terrain.

Case Study 2: Perceptual Analogues and the Axis of Translation Map: GIS Data_scape and Everchanging Frame Perceptual analogues encompass two sides of the continuum of visuality: 1) the point of view from which we perceive a space and 2) the geometric projection used to present space to us. Maps manage this continuum through the axis of translation and the projection selected for the geometric space in the map. remember the axis of translation is the point of view of the observer determined by the mapmaker. What google earth calls ‘street view’ is an embodied view taking into account human binocular vision similar to perspective.

Using google earth software one can transition from the overhead view (vertical axis of translation) to the street view (horizontal axis of translation) to simulate an experience of the continuum along the axes of translation. Maps can combine views, but generally mapmakers select either a birds-eye view or a perspectival view when accounting for an experience of space in the map (I say this cautiously given the many possibilities in mapping). Unlike the perspectival view, the geometry of orthographic projection assumes light rays are parallel offering an infinite set of points from which the surface of the representation will be viewed—think of a typical planimetric view or roadway map. Isometric views are impossible to experience and give us a geometrically distorted view of an object (Figure 4.27). The playful ‘mapping’ of occupied spaces in a room by Michael Schwartz (Figure 4.28) is not really a map but more an image illustrating possible perspectival points of view in a room.

To qualify as a map he would need to point our attention to how the space is distorted.

The following maps focus on different mechanisms for spatial perception. The first map uses ideas from ecological perceptions to describe a scene using a gIS dataset. The second map uses two axes of translation: the plan-view ordered by orthographic geometries and the perceptual view organized by the perspectival optics of the camera lens capturing what the observer sees.

GIS Data_scape Mapping lysa Janssen asked how we perceive a scene using theories of perception from ecological psychology. her questions pertain to how we see: 1) what is the spatio-perceptual mechanism of seeing and 2) how to show what we are actually ‘seeing’ through analogy. To understand the context of the first question Janssen turned to classical theories of perception in cognitive psychology. This was a reasoned approach to the problem but it required learning something about perception in cognitive psychology. There are two general ways to think about perception in cognitive psychology. one is an indirect theory where reality is rationalized through perception and the second is a theory of direct perception put forward originally by James J. gibson. Indirect theories have a longer history. They posit perception is learned through unconscious inference allowing us to seek a meaningful explanation for

4.28 A photo-montage of the possible positions of a figure in a room. Michael Schwartz, Washington University in St. Louis, 2001.

4.27 Isometric drawing from below. Auguste Choisy, Palatin, Courtesy of Colloque Centenaire Auguste Choisy.

Case Studies the patterns in the stimulations in the world. In this modality perception is a construction not a mechanistic process assuming sensory information alone is insufficient to attain perception—we are equipped with some kind of ‘intelligence to learn how to comprehend their meaning.’54 Perception is more than sensation—it is a cognitive process requiring some kind of intellectual capacity for us to process the information we receive through the senses—sensory data alone is insufficient; it is dependent on the perceiver. gibson proposed an alternative approach to visual information acquisition: a direct or realist view where the environment is understood to provide all the information necessary to specify its properties.55 gibson’s ecological approach emphasizes the direct experience of the world mapped by the visual cortex. In this model sensory stimuli are sufficient for us to act—we acquire meaning directly from the perceptual process—it doesn’t occur after we receive the information from our senses. This is gibson’s notion of affordances: our perceptions afford our actions. For instance gibson argued the transforming optic array contains meaning like the pattern of light created by a tree stump indicates it is ‘sit-able on’.56 Direct perception emphasizes the importance of the quality of the stimuli over cognitive processing. gradients, optic flow, information as input (not stimuli) and the senses are understood as a ‘perceptual system.’ Memory is past experience inferred as attunement and the differentiation between stimuli rather than a recording of narrative experience.

Using a gibsonian approach, Janssen’s GIS Data-Scape map proposes that the raw data of a visual image (photograph) be treated as a visual field of color projected using gIS software (Figure 4.29). Using a single image of a scene (Figures 4.30–4.32) she explored the quality of the visual stimuli. The first step in the process is the pixellation of the original image, then pixels are converted into bands of color abstracting the image to its most basic color data. A second iteration converted pixellated images to a set of densities depending on a preselected range of values from light to dark. The new mapping translates one set of data (light) to a three-dimensional landscape (see Figure 4.30) focusing on the attenuation and differentiation 4.29 Single image remapped as color bars and divided into quadrants. Lysa Janssen, GIS Datascape Mapping, Harvard, 2006.

4.30 Analysis of isolated image with interpolated ‘visual’ landscape. Lysa Janssen (and author), GIS Datascape, Harvard, 2006.

Figure 1. A photograph of an outdoor street scene.

The semantic content of the image is less important for this experiment than the contrast of light and dark, the clarity of edge conditions, and the implication that the perceiver is moving, in this case, in a forward direction.

There are two significant issues addressed in this experiment in visual perception : 1) how you are seeing, and 2) how to measure what you are seeing.

In the experimental version shown above, the theoretical framework uses an ecoLogicaL appoach to perception. In an ecological approach the emphasis is a direct perception where the environment is understood to provide all of the information necessary to specify it's properties . Indirect approaches associated with Rene Oescartes assume that our perceptual awareness of reality comes from representations we have already formed in our minds about reality. In contrast, the ecological approach developed by psychologist James J. Gibson, assumes that the environment contains all of the necessary properties for perceving visual information.

Perceiving these properties is then a function of how well we can detect them.

According to Gibson, the properties available in a scene are not explicable in terms of general laws, meaning they are not mechanistic, but rather, are produced from a dynamic interaction between perceiver and context . The ecological approach is therefore an ecology of perceiver-environment reciprocity.

In this model of perception the environment is a continuous background surface that provides visual structure. Is is the "ground"

against which the perceiver gauges their own position in the scence. As a perceiver moves through the environment they are using visual information relative to the background or context to determine size, distance, and shape values.

The experiment is designed to test a series of ways to measure the background or context (Question 2} .

Using a Geospatial Information System approach to the visual data of thes scene we calibrated the depth of the scence as a function of lights and darks according to their tonal value and color. The view of the scence was pixelated to simplify the visual field and allow the data to be tagged with multiple properties: 1) color/tonal value, and 2)

z-axis information. The values were inverted for depth. The higher the z-value the lighter the field.

and wire-frame overlay.

The resulting visual mappping (Figures 2-7) are a measurable tonal field or landscape that enable us to look at the scene as a continuous background with incremental changes according to the quality of light and dark, the overall perception of depth and the outlines of shapes.

This experiemnt is an early example of testing how to measure depth and object shapes using tonal values in a visual field.

We are using GIS capablities to tag data and create a continuous data-field to test ways to understand how to calibrate visual scenes. Several assumptions are embedded in the process: 1)

there are few distortions in the original visual field, and 2)

the percevier is human.

4.32 Lysa Janssen, Reclassification of color into values of lightness and darkness. GIS Datascape Mapping, Harvard, 2006.

Case Studies of the visual stimuli. This heightens the edges between tonal densities as part of the visual scene and identifies what gibson would consider the direct visual environment. This is not a strictly ecological approach as intended by gibson as it doesn’t account for movement or optic flow; rather it plays with visual stimuli as a field of view and demonstrates the malleability of using gIS technologies to explore the way perception operates. Janssen didn’t apply her study directly to a design project; rather her map suggests a mode of visualizing what our perceiving systems may be ‘seeing’ which in itself is useful in design.

Everchanging Frame Daniel Alonso’s map explicitly complemented his studio project. The site was located on lincoln road in Miami, Florida, considered one of the most successful urban pedestrian streets in the U.S. Everchanging Frame: One Lincoln Road Experience (Figure 4.33) is a folio of fourteen sheets documenting the perceptual experience of both sides of the street.

4.33 Daniel Alonso, Everchanging Frame: One Lincoln Road Experience, Florida International University, 2013.

Case Studies This map is about the qualities of a perceptual experience in an urban environment. The analysis is a qualitative mapping describing the spatial frame made by the permanent structures along lincoln road, including buildings, follies, green walls, benches, fountains, hard canopies and sculpture. The dataset (Figure 4.34) is a compilation of 110 photographs taken at 20 pace intervals. overlaying the frames is a conceptual screening of the positive and negative space of lincoln road in perspective enforcing the compressive and expansive quality of the actual space. The mapped frames revealed interesting parameters for thinking about the way to organize the limits of what we see on a street.

First, the frames on lincoln rd. change from block to block, which explains in part why the experience doesn’t feel repetitive, even though much of the materiality, dimensions of sidewalks and plazas are similar. Second, the view from one side to the other on each block varies considerably depending on the direction of travel. Similar to some of lynch and Appleyard’s studies in A View from the Road demonstrating views along paths are asymmetrical offering two distinctly different possible experiences of any single configuration of space. The mapping project was done in support of studio work and initially Alonso approached it as a means to document the site; however, once he perceived the site this way it was hard to not ‘think’ frames. The challenge in the translation to design was finding the appropriate architectural expression of the idea—not using the visual data in the map literally, but understanding it as an abstraction of a principle using architectural language (walls for instance). This requires the designer begin the project using the discourse of these elements—in other words if you look at something ‘wall-like’ operating in the vertical or perspectival axis you have to start thinking about the design problem with perceptual representations of ‘wall-like’ objects—you can’t start in plan, but rather in section or perspective. remember—these drawing types are maps using projection. Designing in the same cartographical modality supports the translation from one language (the language of the original map) to another (the language in the design discipline).

Case Study 3: Thematic Mapping and Indexing Map: Distributing Dynamics and Chronotope of Babel Thematic maps often explore an idea in a geographic context. The spatial organization of the DnA by Watson and Crick is a thematic map of the human body organized as the proteins in a double helix. Thematic maps provide information about a geographic locale, help us recognize spatial patterns and compare patterns in different datasets or conditions.

They are univariate if the non-location data is of the same kind (proteins in the human DnA)

and multivariate if they include two datasets or more like population density and energy use (see Chapter 2 for a detailed discussion of thematic maps). The following maps use the thematic map to various ends. The index is an important element in any thematic map.

Indices are used to point from one set of conditions to another—the index is literally an indicator, sign or measure of something, but in the map it helps signify content and work in much the same way it does in language. Indexing takes the signs of one system and draws parallels or analogies to other systems. Familiar tropes like the idea of ‘change over time’ expressed through the growth of a tree serve to explain. Just as a tree grows, an

4.34 Plate 3, Daniel Alonso, Everchanging Frame: One Lincoln Road Experience, Florida International University, 2013.

Case Studies idea blooms or a story unfolds. The rate of the tree growth serves as an index to the other quantities or qualities described: either the aging process, the passing of time in a novel, or the idea something is becoming more complex. The index can be a powerful starting point for a map as it allows the mapper to make inferences between conditions in a spatial context otherwise more difficult to manage. like the idea of the chronotope discussed in gia Wolff’s map—without indexing the idea of an infinite set of hexagonal shaped rooms to the narrative of the short story it would be difficult to ‘see’ the time-space relationship suggested by the chronotope of Borges story.

Map: Distributing Dynamics Redistributing the Dynamics of the World by Siliang Fu explores the relationship between time and distance by reordering the world according to the time needed to travel from one location to another via airplane (Figure 4.35). Buckminster Fuller’s AirOcean World Map (1946) proposed a similar reordering of the world (Figure 4.36) based on the transport of goods and people through airplanes. It is worth looking at this closely to understand Fu’s proposition. The continents are reorganized according to the shortest routes for air travel using air-ocean currents. First published in Life magazine, the Dymaxion map was part of a photographic essay titled ‘Life Presents r. Buckminster Fuller’s Dymaxion World.’ The article included examples of ways to assemble the map as a ‘three-dimensional approximation of a globe or laid out as a flat map, with which the world may be fitted together and rearranged to illuminate special aspects of its geography.’57 The first version was a projection based on the cuboctahedron. The 1954 Airocean World Map used a regular icosahedron. Fuller argued air travel replaced ocean travel and our worldview reflects this change. The Dymaxion map does not have an ‘up’ or ‘down’ as most world maps including the Mercator projection familiar to Western culture. Fuller believed the Dymaxion corrects other maps informed by cultural biases. The Dymaxion shows earth’s landmasses as ‘one island’ with less relative distortion of actual sizes than other maps. Fuller insisted the best way for viewers introduced to the map was with their home country presented in the middle. But where Fuller used a new projection of the three-dimensional globe as sections of an icosahedron, Fu’s map simply moves the existing landmass as a function of the time to distance relationship based on air travel and telecommunications. The map offers three versions of the world: past, present, and future. In his new world map the world is not only perceptually smaller but also literally shrinking.58 For Fu the map (see Figure 4.35) began as a separate study from his design project.

he wanted to understand difference between actual distance and perceived geographic distance. The world map was a convenient object of analysis. Sufficient information was available for him to find data at-hand about travel distances, communication technologies and so forth. his map is a straightforward proposition looking at the relationship of travel times between points on the globe in three eras: the Industrial revolution represented by the travel time in the 1900, the Information Age in the year 2000, and a projection of change according to the current rate of change in the year 2030. To understand time he compared rates of travel using the most common means (ship, train, airplane) for the time period between major cities on the globe. The bottom two thirds of the map show the rates of time

4.35 Siliang Fu, Redistributing the Dynamics of the World, Washington University in St. Louis, 2010.

4.36 Buckminster Fuller patented the Dymaxion map in 1946. The projection used for this map translates the globe into sections of an icosahedron maintaining the relative proportional integrity of the landmass and oceans. The map above indicates various numbers of possible combinations. The AirOcean map is a slightly different projection than the original Dymaxion, but essentially the same. It privileges air travel over ocean travel revealing a new view of the modern world. Eric Gaba, Dymaxion map, after Buckminster Fuller.

Case Studies and geographical locations associated with the most frequent destinations. The top category ‘perception’ is a reconfigured map of the globe showing the time compression occurring as our means of travel increase in speed and reduce the time to destination. The map projects that by 2030 our perceptions of global connectedness will be significantly different than the previous 130 years. one of the unforeseen outcomes of his map demonstrated the degree of connectivity linked to speed in general transportation. The number of major destinations increases significantly—vast swaths of Mongolia excluded from the current global economy are connected by 2001. The change from 1900 to 2000 indicates less an opening of new territories, but an intensification of the number of people and goods being exchanged. his data does not make a distinction between people or goods, but only accounts for numbers of connections. From an historical point of view the map underscores how early globalization, began assuming the numbers did not spike in 1900, but increased along a more or less logarithmic curve. Information as ‘goods’ is the next step in global connectivity. Telematics potentially links even the most remote domains.

once the relationship between time, space, and perception was calibrated in Fu’s map he applied the same principles to the program of his studio project. The program was analogous to the activities or events in the spaces of the building. he looked at the density of vertical and horizontal connections between the building site and its surrounding urban environment (Figure 4.37) based on the speed and accessibility of vehicular and pedestrian traffic movements, activities, and vertical location in the space around the site. his original map analysis implies that air or ocean travel occurs in different locations relative to the spatial volume of the globe, but his urban analysis highlights this property. he distributed building programs across multiple sites (Figure 4.38) including the original building plot to distribute the program according to the perception of its connectivity to the urban activities. The architecture became an urban landscape problem organized by events unfolding through a particular set of blocks in the city (Figure 4.39). he proposed using street signage, visual cues, materiality, and activities or program to extend the perception of his building into the urban scape. The resulting project re-distributes people and goods broadly throughout the site and surrounding context.

Chronotope of Babel gia Wolff’s Chronotope of Babel is a thematic mapping of the short story ‘The library of Babel’ by Argentine author Jorge luis Borges. In the story the universe is presented as a vast library containing only 410-page books. Borges imagines the spatial organization of the eponymous library as an infinite field of interlocking hexagonal rooms. Wolff’s map (Figure 4.40) chronicles the time-space of the narrative of the story or what literary theorist Mikhail Bakhtin designated a chronotope.59 The term describes the spatio-temporal link controlling the structure of any given narrative or speech act. All stories take place somewhere and at some time. In the case of the ‘library of Babel,’ the narrative unfolds in relation to the hexagonal units of a library as described by Borges’ narrator, the ‘traveller.’60 As the traveller moves through the myriad rooms of the library, the narrative of the story unfolds in relation to the intimate spaces of the library rooms. The example often used by Bakhtin to explain this relationship is a contrast between Tolstoy and Dostoevsky. In Tolstoy’s War and Peace,

4.37 Mapping of horizontal and vertical connections on the site by density. Siliang Fu, Redistributing the Dynamics of the World, Washington University in St. Louis, 2010.

4.38 Traditional map of adjacent programs by type. Siliang Fu, Redistributing the Dynamics of the World, Washington University in St. Louis, 2010.

4.39 Time-based mapping of site relationships using larger sections of Manhattan.

The density or layering of the program was determined by activity and relative connectivity based on 1)

actual distance and 2) perceived distance. Siliang Fu, Redistribution of Urban Dynamics in Manhattan, New York, Washington University in St.

Louis, 2010.

Case Studies the action of the book takes place in large spaces: ballrooms, battlefields and public plazas.

By contrast in The Idiot by Dostoevsky the narrative action of the novel takes place in intimate spaces: the foyer of a house, the hallway or on a porch. The link between the time and place a novelist uses to tell the story is the chronotope of a given book.

The underlying layer of Wolff’s map is the text of the book ‘folded’ corresponding to the overlap of the events of the story linked to the space in which they occur (Figure 4.41 and 4.42). The frame of the map is the limit of Borges essay or the internal time of the story in relation to its imagined space. Unlike the text in which the ordering principle is language and syntax, the map is ordered by the hexagonal figure of the library and the sequence of the story as told by the narrator. It was important neither time nor space was privileged, this is part of the definition of the chronotope according to Bakhtin, deemed important by the mapmaker. Ultimately the map is a translation of Borges dystopic world in which neither space, time, or language are stable.

Case Study 4: Catalogues, Taxonomies, and Time The Ideal Villa, Natural Forces, The Eidos of the Cup, and Atlases In each of the following maps the idea of time is predominant whether of short or long duration, diachronic or synchronic. The Ideal Villa considers the seasonal conditions of light in an architectural space. This map catalogues and organizes the effect of these changes as taxonomies of the qualities of the light in the space. Taxonomies and catalogues are maps of a milieu of objects in any given set. Taxonomy is the science of classification (from the 4.40 Gia Wolff, Chronotope of Babel, Harvard.

Case Studies greek, taxis, means order). In practice, taxonomies were originally applied to organisms (think of linnaean classification) but their use in a wider sense applies to any set of objects or concepts. Taxonomies are usually hierarchical, but this is not a requirement; the term taxonomy applies to relationship schemes like network structures. They can be in the form of lists, trees structures, polyhierarchies, matrices, facets, and system maps.61 In principle, taxonomies like any order are not naturalized but constructed. library catalogues are a taxonomy—they organize the taxa or items in the library—in this case, books. on the one hand this can be fairly straightforward—one isn’t likely to confuse a book about mathematics with something on music, but on deeper inspection this may not be true. Music and mathematics are generally not considered ontologically the same, but they share significant overlap epistemologically. For instance, a book may be about proportioning systems and their relation to early music. The librarian in one library may locate it in music theory while another librarian would catalogue the same book under histories of mathematics. Denis Wood in The Power of Maps addresses this regarding the interests in maps selected from: [the] vast storehouse of knowledge about the earth the map will represent, these interests are embodied in the map as presences and absences. every map shows this … but not that, and every map shows what it show this way … but not the other. not only is this inescapable but it is precisely because of this interested selectivity—this choice of word or sign or aspect of the world to make a point—the map is enabled to work.62

4.41 (opposite)

The Traveler text is an underlay of the final map. The following explanation accompanies the map: ‘In the established set of hierarchical relationships in the map the most dominant one exists between the Librarian (hexagon) and the Traveler (babble/babel).

Within each of those categories are a series of other relationships of varying degrees of importance. The Librarian’s role is to discover meaning from a stationary point, while the Traveler’s role is to find meaning through a journey. The overall format is duplicated in any cardinal direction as a repeatable pattern. From a two dimensional axis the repetitive quality emphasizes the endlessness spatiality of the library and the longstanding interdependency between the Traveler and the Librarian. All of the other relationships in the map are not repeatable in the same infinite way. The relationship between the Librarian and the Traveler most strongly reveals the time/space relationship in the story. Though they may physically exist in different states of being—one static and one moving—they both share the same reason for being: they are both trying to discover meaning. For example, it is important to the Traveler that the Librarian stays in one place so they can chase their ideas and have their footsteps traced. This could not be possible without a stationary point of reference. By the same token it is important for the Librarian to know the Traveler is searching for the proof of meaning in places only read about.

In total, the narrator speaks of twelve characters, including himself. Three of them are Travelers (Purifiers, Inquisitors, Pilgrims) and the rest are Librarians (Narrator, Father, Man, Idealists, Mystics, Philosophers). Varying texts within twelve hexagons represents each character. The Babel/babble text representative of the Traveler is freed to move around the page. This text sorts its letters out to reveal legible words. Not necessarily meaningful words, since meaning is still unknown, but nevertheless readable. At other times the axis rotates and allows for the text to move to the foreground, background or stay plumb.

Because this text has freedom of movement (it folds in response to the narrative space) it is used to help define the standard parameters of a map such as parts of the frame or the index.’ Gia Wolff, Chronotope of Babel, Harvard University, 2006.

4.42 Repetitive base.

4.42–4.46 The rigidity in composition of the base layer is important because it speaks of the inherent and infinite relationship between Librarians and Travelers. However, it does not take into account that in the novel they repeat in the z-axis. The line drawing of additional hexagons, both in the foreground and in the background, implies a z-direction. Offsetting of the secondary set of hexagons implies a changing of order, perhaps a new-found order that to some degree seems the hope of both Librarians and Travelers. Similarly, the blending of hexagons sporadically seen in the map is the mapmaker’s interpretation of the extreme unknown; it is not described, but sharing the knowledge of the possibility for such far off imagination is possible. Time and space are expressed in the infinite two-dimensional axis of the map. Because of the equal compositional placement of the symbols, questions arise about the hexagons in the foreground and the babble in the background. The leveling of the figure–ground relationship plays an optical Babel/babble trick and allows for the interdependency or the expression of the chronotopic qualities of the characters. In other words it would be impossible for the hexagons to exist without the space where Babel resides, so it would be impossible for babble to exist without making the space for the hexagons. The map frame indulges this line of thinking as it transitions from the lower portion of the map dominated by the ground (babble) and the figure (hexagon) dominating the frame at the top, playing into the notions of figure/ground, foreground/background. Lastly, the purpose of duplicating the standard format of the map on the right hand side of the page expresses the possibility of order similar to Borges’s conclusion. He says, ‘The Library is unlimited and cyclical. If an eternal traveler were to cross it in any direction, after centuries he would see the same volumes were repeated in the same disorder (which, thus repeated, would be an order: the Order)’ (emphasis added). Case Studies Taxonomies and catalogues as maps are of this order: the inclusion of this and exclusion of that allowing us to see hidden relations, concealed characteristics or principles and potentially new aspects of a familiar landscape or idea.

The Ideal Villa The Ideal Villa (Figure 4.47) by raymond Chau and ruogu liu addresses a lack in the research regarding the use of the mathematical proportions in Palladian architecture. Chau and liu’s case study was the Villa rotonda outside of Padua by Andrea Palladio. like other renaissance humanists, Palladio used the golden Section and other proportional systems to manage room configurations. Palladio published a treatise, I quattro libri dell’architettura (1570), detailing the principles for rules of ratio and proportion where: beauty will derive from a graceful shape and the relationship of the whole to the parts, and of the parts among themselves and to the whole, because buildings must appear complete and well-defined bodies, of which one member matches another and all the members are necessary for what is required.”63 This was a further articulation of ideas expressed in previous treatises by Vitruvius and Alberti.64 For many renaissance scholars and artists ‘the human body derives all measures and their denominations and in it is found all and every ratio and proportion by which god reveals the innermost secrets of nature.’65 The question underlying the student exploration was whether Palladio’s assertion of the proportional system was ‘merely a mathematical representation of the humanist ideal, or whether they held a deeper purpose in affecting the functional aspect of human experience in the rooms or the building as a whole.’66 They hypothesized that the rules of proportion applied by Palladio in his buildings were reflections of the mathematical ideal, but manipulated to optimize human experience such as the perception of light in the building. The methodology for testing meant mapping the relationship between the ideal proportions of Palladio’s systems and actual proportions of the rooms of the Villa rotunda.

Their investigation included lighting studies examining how proportions of selected rooms in the Villa rotunda affected ambient lighting (Figure 4.48). The lighting experiment (Figure 4.49) examined changes in the room’s length/width ratio, height, and ceiling type affecting natural light levels. Comparing maps of the lighting conditions for the actual and typical ideal proportions employed by Palladio underscored changes in room proportions in select rooms and had a strong impact on the illumination of interior spaces. Their real discovery using the maps was to reveal that Palladio adjusted room proportions to maximize for light even if they were outside of the mathematical model of ideal proportion.67 They demonstrated that human need informed the application of ideal proportion in the Villa rotunda.

Natural Forces The maps in this section look at natural processes and time. Feixion Yu explores the expansion of the cellular structure of the cactus plant. The second map, Wind Analysis on 11th St., was part of a quick exercise done over a weekend with four students. Vipavee

The studies of light penetration into the rooms of the villa reveal that Palladio changed proportions of rooms to allow for maximum lighting. The last case is an exploration of the idea of the atlas as a collection of phenomenal experiences of place. The Atlas Mar project was pursued in studio with a number of students. Responsive Structures: Model of Cactaceae Feixion Yu’s map (Figure 4.50) models changes in the cellular structure of cactus skin of the desert cactus prevalent in the southwestern United States. The first observation he made for a desert site used in his landscape architecture studio was about ‘change over time in nature’ as cacti change in relation to the amount of light and temperature relatively rapidly in a twenty- four-hour period once exposed to rain. he next modeled a simple open–close process using the cactus skin as a prototype because the action is symmetrical and in direct relationship to light, available water, and temperature. Most cacti have thick, fleshy stems with a waxy skin surface. The stem acts as a reservoir to store water and the tough skin keeps the water from evaporating. Surfaces of most cactuses expand and contract to accommodate changes in the amount of water. This is what Yu modeled on a computer. once he had the basic pattern of change in the cell he created a computer model that converted the data into a façade or wall.

The beauty of his analysis is it didn’t mimic the cells directly, but translated the open–close action at the cellular level to an architectural language with thickness and depth. The wall uses a bladder system when filled with air forces the units into a closed configuration (see left side of ‘façade’) and when deflated (see right) opens the unit again.

Wind Analysis on 11th St.

The data for the wind analysis in this map was rather ingeniously planned. Using a low- technology approach, students stationed a series of balloons affixed to stings on the site.

The location was on Miami Beach in Florida in a sliver site within blocks of the oceanfront.

Balloons were organized in rows and columns using a numbering system to indicate their positions (Figure 4.51). Student photographs documented the position of the balloon markers. They used cameras stationed at the same position along the western edge of the site for each row. Using perspective they were able to locate four points along each balloon string. The known dimensions of the strings and the measured distance between balloons created a mesh model in the computer based on the deflection of each of the points (see top figure in the map). The map doesn’t show all of the data, but a few of the key components of the method. Shown is part of a set of sheets in a larger document addressing site conditions. The computational mesh captured the wind conditions only for the duration the cameras caught the positions of the balloons. The site analysis was a group project. Students used the resulting understanding in their individual projects and were able to draw different conclusions. This particular map is interesting for the novel way students captured the initial data. It is thoughtful, if imprecise, and demonstrates how with

Case Studies limited tools it is possible to produce a robust map. They were careful and deliberate—the map is a good example of Toulmin’s notion that ‘to establish the form of a regularity … only a few careful observations are needed.’69 I wouldn’t compare these observations with the precision of calibrated instrumentation, but given the imprecision of their subject, wind, this may not be as underdetermined as one may think.

The Eidos of the Cup The map by Vipavee kunavichayanont The Eidos of the Cup explores the Platonic Idea or ideal of an everyday object following on the same philosophy informing the Palladian notion of the ideal in the map of the Villa rotonda. The idea of an object for Plato was its original model. What we sense in this reality is only a copy of an original. When we look at an object in this world we awaken a memory or anamnesis of the idea, or ideal object in our minds.

Art mimics objects in our world that are already copies of an ideal, thus by default all art objects are copies of a copy of a copy. This makes aesthetic judgment a lesser discipline than, say, philosophy that represents through language ideals or the truths of our reality.

The map is a both a catalogue of images over time and a commentary on the notion of the copy. kunavichayanont hypothesized that staring at or listening to an object activates anamnesis and reveals the ideal ‘-ness’ of the object. She chose for her subject a quotidian object—a simple white paper cup (Figure 4.52).

The map is a catalogue of images of a paper cup over a five-minute time period. The paper cup was photographed, then the photograph printed on a transparent film and framed in a slide mount. The image was projected (Figure 4.53) then rephotographed, the process repeated to include enough images of the cup at one image per second for five sixty-second periods. each image is a reproduction of a reproduction, a copy of a copy. A similar procedure was used to record and calibrate the sound associated with the projection of the cup image. The map was a video of images projected as a series of overlays and their attendant ambient sound maps. The final compilation of image and sound (Figure 4.54)

resonates with the essence of the cup – its ‘-ness’. The final map is a side-by-side sequence of all of the images and the sound files (Figures 4.55 and 4.56).

Atlases Atlases are generally geographical—they map the earth, or a portion of the earth, they describe economic conditions, political boundaries or populations. But atlases are made for any subject: plants, birds, stars, history and human anatomy. recent atlases are published in multimedia format. Most map historians use a loose definition: an atlas indicates a collection of maps bound in a book. Map historian Peter van der krogt’s definition rests on a strict bibliographic understanding of an atlas as a group of maps issued or bound together as a book with the express purpose of using graphical information.70 For the aims of the maps shown in Atlas Mar both definitions apply. The teaching team used the atlas format to ground a studio project with architecture and landscape architecture students entitled, Atlas Mar: Re-imagining Biscayne Bay. The team included two architecture faculty, one landscape architect, a marine biologist and a poet-in-residence at university.

The studio envisioned the future of Biscayne national Park by conceiving it as a place

Case Studies where we re-examine our relationship with the natural world. The studio methodology included robust mapping and cataloging exercises aimed at critically situating the user within the context of Biscayne national Park’s natural environment by conceiving of it as a paradigm for the new national park. The studio title, Atlas Mar, reﬂected our intention design research be catalogued through the map. We introduced students to the basic principles of mapmaking in the ﬁrst weeks of the studio. They studied map types, including ichnographic and thematic, and photogrammetry or aerial mapping technologies.

exercises targeted a healthy tension between a rational understanding of the national park and a poetic interpretation of its ineffable qualities through mapping. Students gathered combined quantitative and qualitative data over the fifteen-week semester (see Figures 1.25 to 1.31). Additional quantitative analysis included comparing existing maps by making new maps, open public polling, comparative analysis of existing parks, and on-site experiential documentation through thematic maps. All maps were organized into an atlas prior to the ﬁnal student projects.

Students developed an ordering system or taxonomy of objects found on the site serving as the initial vehicle for developing a design language (see Figure 1.28). later they negotiated site constraints with a series of qualitative impressions inﬂuencing tangible design proposals. The taxonomies were a starting point for analysis of the site. Depending on the class of objects, their location or particular properties, students generated an analytical map of the particular characteristics of the context. examples ranged from tactile, visual, or aural properties, to perceptual or cognitive analysis (Figures 1.27 and 1.28). The resulting maps explored their observations of the site. The root of the proposal (Figures 4.57 and 4.58) lies in a system facilitating communication and circulation of ideas, data, research, and science through open and programmed aquatic-based platforms.

More interesting is the implicit breakdown of the park boundary through the technological mediation. Treating the park as part of the city suggests the difference of landscape and cityscape is one of degree not kind. Parks of the twenty-first century won’t be understood at a distance or remove, but potentially as part of a complete ecology where the city is a constituent part. our ecological acts are not in isolation: what we do in a place telescopes in our mediated view to another.71 Case Study 5: Images of the City through Movement Automotive Perceptions_×7 and Freeway Camouflage The following maps emphasize movement or change in a physical or conceptual state. In these maps geographical phenomenon are perceived in relation to movement—in the first, movement of the car through a terrain and, in the second, movement over a terrain in a car on a highway. The observer’s point-of-view is key to understanding how these maps relate to the representation of context. There is a tradition of itinerary maps in the history of cartography. The roman emperor Caracalla used some of the earliest itinerary maps; such journeys required staging for the entire imperial household, military escorts and officials and their families.72 one of the earliest examples is the Peutinger map, an early roman road map discussed in Chapter 2 (see note 31). other itinerary maps include navigational

The actual experience of the park for the individual park goer remains largely untouched. It is the experience of the park as a digital representation to outsiders that shifts. Case Studies charts made by the Spanish and Portuguese sailors called portolan charts, derived from the Italian word portolano for a collection of written sailing directions. This nomenclature stresses the way charts are assumed to complement written accounts made by sailors.73 These maps note the most direct navigational routes between known locales and, in this sense, all possible itineraries are shown on a single map, unlike itinerary maps typically read from left to right or unrolled and viewed in sections. Isolarii or the so-called ‘island maps’ were drawn in plan and in elevation (not usually together), thus giving the reader a view of the coastlines as they arrived from the sea. The notion a map can describe an experience is often present in maps of movement: time unfolds or is part of an experiential unfolding (like sailing or driving from one place to another). The following student maps extend similar observations about the qualities of ‘arriving to’ and ‘seeing over’ a locale to build an understanding of the experience applied to the design problem, specifically a building. The reader should keep in mind similar approaches when working on the design of interior or landscape condition.

Automotive Perceptions_×7 Brendan Willis’s Automotive Perceptions_×7 is an itinerary map cataloguing seven possible paths to a particular location: the site for his project (Figure 4.59). The mapping began with a video diary of the seven automobile approaches to the site taken from a moving car (because the site was not amenable to pedestrian traffic). The camera location (approximately 6ʺ) to the left of the driver was the same for each video. Video stills (Figure 4.60) noted time of travel and length of trip allowing the mapmaker to calculate the distance travelled between buildings based on the speed of the car. Willis’s observation built upon the work of the urban designer kevin lynch. he looked at lynch’s maps for the Boston loop in A View from the Road (1964, with Donald Appleyard).74 lynch made a series of maps recalibrating the city as a series of possible texts based on an observer’s frame of view. In his seminal work The Image of the City (1960) lynch, building on the work of the psychologist e. C.

Tolman, proposed we construct cognitive maps of the places we inhabit. By altering the visual landscape of a place the urban designer would in effect change the cognitive map of the user. Several of the projects in A View from the Road, notably the Boston loop proposal, use the cognitive perception of an ideal everyday commuter determined by a series of studies that lynch did with graduate students at MIT.

lynch’s methodology included using architecture students and their colleagues. Students were asked to drive the highway in pairs with one student focused on documenting what they saw and then redrawing the experience based on what they remembered (Figure 4.61).

Student researchers simplified the images to highlight landmarks, boundaries, and paths observable from the highway (Figure 4.62). Willis used a video camera to document all of the roads to the site, at first repeating lynch’s methods, but soon realizing his study offered another opportunity. Automotive Perceptions projects the viewer’s experience onto the context through a careful mapping of the perceptual distortions. What the designer gains is a measured understanding of the cognitive map of the site. his insight was to realize views of fixed objects (buildings, infrastructure, signage) distort when seen from a moving vehicle. We infer from the dataset, the speed of the car and the time traveled, how long an object ‘stays in view.’ his map documents a redrawn set of fixed objects according

4.60 Shown are a series of stills taken from video record of one of the itineraries for Automotive Perceptions_×7, Brendan Willis, Washington University in St. Louis, 2010.

Case Studies to this relationship. The base map (drawn in black, see Figure 4.59) assumes a zero-point of movement or stasis. The base was redrawn seven times (in color) according to the new ‘temporal scale’ of the context buildings. The temporal time is the amounts of time buildings appear in the fixed frame of vision or the time buildings are present in the video frame corresponding to the driver’s view through the windshield. The new building profiles are juxtaposed to the neutrality of the path (road) to focus the map on the viewer’s perception of the building scape.

Freeway Camouflage Freeway Camouflage by Melissa Blair is a perceptual mapping based on a series of videos (Figure 4.64) taken from a moving car. The original dataset consists of a series of video stills reduced to high-contrast images. The reduction of the images to contrasting light and dark focuses the map-reader’s attention to the patterning of the existing visual landscape.

like animal camouflage manipulating the visual field to conceal the fact something is being concealed, this phenomenal camouflage is not about hiding a building, but concealing the method of its re-projection as an object in the site. These visual field distortions underscore the notion of camouflage as a map used to order morphisms between perception and space. Under this rubric camouflage is full-scale mapping: it maps the unreal onto the real.

The same principle was used in World War I, and to some extent World War II: paint measures applied to naval ships (Figure 4.65). Dazzle camouflage as it was known is based on ideas originating with the english Vorticist art movement. led by the experiments of norman Wilkinson, dazzle painting emerged from the combined efforts of Wilkinson, Alfred Coburn, and edward Wadsworth, but shared features of the group’s collective work. In a strange turn of fate their aesthetic manifesto published in the ill-fated journal Blast and the short-lived artistic movement produced its most important work meeting the utilitarian 4.61 Tokaido dochu ezo byobu, an itinerary map of the Tokaido Road from Edo to Kyoto to Osaka, seventeenth century, courtesy of the C. V. Starr East Asian Library, University of California, Berkeley.

4.62 This analysis from the Boston Loop proposal by Kevin Lynch and Donald Appleyard in A View from the Road identifies visual interest or ‘major landmarks’ (e.g. Prudential Center Building) and boundary conditions between districts, ‘points of decision’ and ‘areas of confusion.’ There is a series of similar maps in the study identifying night conditions, sounds and visual ‘excitement.’ Lynch emphasizes the relationship between habitat and urbanism through ‘patterns of organization’ charted from ‘land-use maps, topographic maps, area photographs, and general experience.’ The legend of the map is the phenomenon of the experience of the spatial organization Lynch wants to tease from the existing city. If we understand mapping implies a process by which to infer higher mental processes, then reconstructing spatial relationships also implies the restructuring of cognitive relationships. Lynch intends a remap of the environment in order to alter the cognitive maps of the city’s inhabitants. D. Appleyard, K. Lynch and J. R. Myer, The View from the Road, drawings: 52.

4.63 A series of simplified views showing the sequence from the Boston Loop proposal printed in Kevin Lynch and Donald Appleyard, A View from the Road. This sequence illustrates the cognitive experience of the ‘Crossing’ Lynch associates to the functional zone of ‘Entertainment.’ For Lynch, as for CIAM, the disorientation of the post-industrial city was symptomatic of the disjunction between the subject and the legibility of the urban space. The issue of legibility is also to say ‘identity’ or identification within the ‘mobile and endless labyrinths’ of our cities. For Lynch this identity is pluralist and specific to the American political system, although curiously it is in Europe his book is of more immediate interest perhaps because such diverse theorists as Guy Debord and the English architects Alison and Peter Smithson were exploring similar maps. The ‘psychogeographic’ maps of the Situationist International (see Figure 3.23), and the Smithsons’ London Road Study (1959) bear marked similarities in the articulation of the problem as well as the suggestion a ‘mental map’ is an appropriate representational strategy. The London Road Study refuted the functionalist city. Rather than the familiar endless grid of the modernist city, density in the traffic net focuses activity into clusters of social, therefore, cognitive interaction. D.
