Case Studies needs of war: disguising allied war-ships and merchant vessels from german U-boats. In dazzle painting the whole ship above the water line is painted in bold colors in an angular or curved pattern. The principal idea was to disrupt the silhouette of the ship, much like zebra stripes work in the wild to break down the totality of the zebra’s form. In theory this would make it more difficult for an enemy captain looking through the periscope to gauge the speed, distance, and course needed to direct torpedoes (Figure 4.66). The horizon line relative to the view from the periscope is the datum against which calculations were made.

Although never assessed for its effectiveness at the end of the war, over 4,000 ships were painted like Vorticist paintings, a veritable sailing gallery of artwork.

Melissa Blair’s map was a tool to project an idea about the site as a building in conjunction with programmatic and tectonic needs. her starting point for the project was not program, but site. given her first observation was about the view from the freeway, Blair did not analyze the program, look at cultural context, or even think much about the technical problems associated with the proposal. her first desire was to be curious about the view of the site 4.65 An example of a ship with dazzle paint measures. Official photograph of British Kil class patrol gunboat HMS Kildagan painted in dazzle camouflage, courtesy of the Imperial War Museum.

4.66 The diagram illustrates how dazzle camouflage was intended to confuse an attack from a submarine, ‘Dazzle camouflage effect,’ diagram by Ian Alexander.

4.67 Plan of site with proposed camouflage roof pattern, Melissa Blair, Freeway Camouflage, Washington University in St.

Louis, 2011.

from the highway. She videotaped this view from her car using a fixed camera mounted on the passenger side door. She made numerous passes and selected one in particular with a consistent focus on the site. As shown in the overall site plan (Figure 4.67), the building was situated at the junction of an interstate highway overpass at the intersection of two major roadways, multiple entrances, and off ramps. By reducing video stills to single images she controlled the contrast and brightness, simplifying content of the images to emphasize patterns of light and dark. Blair wasn’t clear about what she sought in the images other than to understand what they may or may not contain. This is a good example of using data, in this case visual data to look for patterns from which a hypothesis or observation develops.

At some point after assembling her dataset and linking it geographically to the site, Melissa realized her interest was in exploring how to hide a very large building (the footprint of the program was over 250,000 square feet) in plain sight (site).

The difficulty of managing this translation from perception back into a visual field and then architecture was the challenge of this exercise. It proved easier at first pass to conceive and control the application of the camouflage from an aerial view than from the ground level, similar to the way military camouflage works better when viewed from above than at eye level. The project tested the idea of a phenomenal camouflage applied not as a building strategy, but a general site/sight strategy. her project addressed experiential camouflage.

The approach developed from a second analysis of the original reduced video stills (Figure 4.68). Manipulated video stills reduced the patterning of the ‘freeway’ landscape to a series of angled planes forming a particular pattern in the landscape similar to the dazzle painting used on the naval ships. given the primary view for the building was from above, she designed the new patterned landscape using the part of the building most visible from the elevated highway: the roof. The folding of the roof panels along with a printed pattern created shade and shadow to match the patterns she observed in the video stills. The

Case Studies military similarly creates camouflage clothing to help soldiers blend into a given terrain yet be recognizable to their fellow soldiers. Using the roof structure as a way to occupy the viewer’s fore and mid-ground view, Blair replicated the effect of the pattern as a visual field for the interior of the project (Figure 4.69). A comparison between the roof assembly and the phenomenal transparency in the interior reveals a coincidence of visual patterning and density (Figure 4.70). It is important to note the formal language of the project wasn’t driven by composition or a particular affinity for the overall geometries, but simply replicating the same visual pattern observed in the original video analysis. Site is privileged in this project over program or tectonics. The structure and materiality of the proposed building are secondary concerns guided by the designer’s desire to manage the visual landscape as a camouflage at the macro-view from the highway. The internal consistency between the original observations by the mapmaker about the view from the road indicates how the mapping helped to define the conceptual approach to the proposal.

Conclusions What do maps do when they work? They make present—they represent—the accumulated thought and labor of the past … about the milieu we simultaneously live in and collaborate on bringing into being. In so doing they enable the past to become part of our living…now…and here. (This is how maps facilitate the reproduction of the culture that brings them into being.)75 one of the best endings for a television program was the last scene in Six Feet Under, a drama about a family of undertakers. In the final scene the youngest daughter of the family starts a cross-country drive to her new home in San Francisco. As a visual metaphor for life, the road, as in kerouac’s On the Road or Chaucer’s Canterbury Tales, is a consistent and familiar trope: the road signifies the journey of life. Time streaming out as a road—unspooling into the vast unknown landscape of our future is a powerful image. kerouac’s story is one of personal growth written in the tire tracks of Sal Paradise and Dean Moriarty as they crisscross the American landscape—the book is a veritable map of places, people, and stories as these two characters journey from new York to San Francisco, to Mexico City, Detroit, and all the small cities inbetween: ‘we streamed through Beaumont, over the Trinity river at liberty, and 4.68 Detail from video stills used in map analysis, Melissa Blair, Freeway Camouflage, Washington University in St. Louis, 2011.

4.69 Model of interior, Melissa Blair, Freeway Camouflage, Washington University in St.

Louis, 2011.

4.70 Detail of final proposed roof structure and patterns made by shadows, material changes and material density, Melissa Blair, Freeway Camouflage, Washington University in St.

Louis.

Case Studies straight for houston.’76 In Six Feet Under, the beauty of the visual metaphor is made more poignant as the deaths of other characters are encountered in a magical calculus where the time of the journey by car to the west coast equates to the lived life of the youngest daughter, until finally we see her death far in the future at the end of the road. What is implicit in the novel structure—time moves forward along a linear path—becomes explicit in the visual media: we ‘see’ each character’s demise while tracking the daughter’s cross-country journey—the visual mapping of every character’s death through one character’s journey of life.

Maps as Denis Wood suggests ‘facilitate the reproduction of the cultures that bring them into being,’77—each map is an accumulation of past maps, traces of the people, places, ideas and worlds that pre-date us and presage us. Maps are living things constantly moving as they absorb and recast the worlds we imagine, destroy, or alter with each new way of being. To use another literary form from science fiction: maps are like the alternative history novel. one of the more difficult of Philip k. Dick’s science-fiction novels is the Man in the High Castle. on first glance it is a dystopian novel where nazi Socialism is victorious after World War II, but unlike most alternate history novels presenting parallel worlds—the one in the novel and ours usually only requiring someone to uncover a way to return to ‘our’ or the ‘right’ version—this novel is denser. There is a novel within the novel suggesting another alternative history similar to our history, but not exactly the same. For instance roosevelt forgoes a second term in office and another president is elected. no story in the novel is our story, but enough familiarity in the timelines exist to disturb the reader—they are similar, but not the same as the world we know. Frederick A. kreuziger posits ‘that this world, described twice, although differently each time,’ by Dick, ‘is exactly the world we know and are familiar with. Indeed it is the only world we know: the world of chance, luck, fate.’78 Maps don’t mirror the complexity of our world: they are somewhat like the world we are in, but not exactly; and somewhat like the world we are making, but only as one possibility out of many. As Denis Cosgrove suggested ‘Mapping remains a way of representing the world; the map remains a visible image of the (or at least a) world.’79 I would suggest the single map is like Dicks’s novel representing many possible worlds all at once and always in process. This is very similar to the paradox faced by designers—to make something from an infinite possible nothing. Maps and mapping are powerful tools for designers in all fields as a way of ordering the phenomenal and ideological worlds we inhabit. Using maps as hypothesis making or pattern analysis makes maps ‘do work’ in the process of design.

12 The full quotation is: ‘lines and planes situated above eye-level should appear to incline downwards as they move away from the spectator; those below eye-level should incline upwards; those to the left should incline to the right, those to the right incline to the left, there should be a sense of division between zones created by the use of vertical and horizontal lines which are not distorted.’ Cennino Cennini, Il Libro dell’Arte, (1507), D. Thompson, Jr (trans.), Dover Publications, new York, 1960: 57.

13 The argument for the topographic need in architecture versus a pictorial perspective typically discussed by art historians is formulated by kuhn in ‘Measured appearances’: 121–22.

14 The comparison of painted perspectives to a future system typical within the art history community is; as kuhn suggests, to ‘elide a momentous discontinuity in the project of representation … The reception of the new invention shows only that it converged with pictorial concerns, not that it met a previously recognized need.’ kuhn, ‘Measured appearances.’

Case Studies 15 Piero della Francesca, Trattato d’Abaco, g. Arrighi (ed.), Domus galilaeana, Pisa, 1970: 233: ‘The rule of Three says that one has to multiply the thing one wants to know about by the thing that is dissimilar to it, and one divides the product by the remaining thing. And the number that comes from this is of the nature of that which is dissimilar to the first term; and the divisor is always similar to the thing that one wants to know about.

For example: seven bracci of cloth are worth nine lire; how much will five bracci be worth?

Multiply the quantity you want to know about by the quantity which seven bracci of cloth are worth—namely, nine. Five times nine makes forty-five. Divide by seven and the result is six and three sevenths.

→ → → 63/7 16 The complete quote is as follows: ‘It is, first, that Quattrocento education laid exceptional value on certain mathematical skills, on gauging and the rule of Three … In the second place, this specialization constituted a disposition to address visual experience, in or out of pictures, in special ways: to attend to the structure of complex forms as combinations of regular geometrical bodies and as intervals comprehensible in series. Because they were practiced in manipulating ratios and in analyzing the volume or surface of compound bodies, they were sensitive to pictures carrying the marks of similar processes. Thirdly there is a continuity between the mathematical skills used by commercial people and those used by the painter to produce the pictorial proportionality and lucid solidity that strike us as so remarkable now.’ M. Baxandall, Painting and Experience in Fifteenth Century Italy, oxford University Press, oxford, 1988: 101.

17 ‘Donatello had no interest in architecture. Filippo told him nothing of his ideas, either because he did not find Donatello apt or because he was not confident of prevailing, seeing more every minute the difficulties confronting him. however, together they made rough drawings of almost all the buildings in tome and in many places beyond the walls, with measurements of widths and heights as far as they were able to ascertain [the latter] by estimation, and also the length, etc. …When possible they estimated the heights [by measuring] from base to base for the height and similarly[they estimated the heights of] the entablatures and roofs from the foundations. They drew the elevations on strips of parchment graphs with numbers and symbols which Filippo alone understood [sic].’ Manetti, Life of Brunelleschi, 52. The mention of the parchment strips here indicates that the routine involved something akin to the surveyors’ rod method, but a kuhn points out, ‘Something more than operational economy is at stake here, however, for the record on the strips is now not merely a collection of discrete measures but a two dimensional array of plotted points, the skeleton of a scaled drawing.’ kuhn, ‘Measured appearances’: 117.

Case Studies 22 Piero wrote three treatises: two explore the nature of mathematical constructions to reality, De Prospectiva Pingendi (1474), the Libellus de quinque corporibus regularibus (1472–73)

and one, a kind of mathematics textbook, the aforementioned Trattato d’Abaco (1450). The book on the five regular bodies, appended to luca Pacioli’s Divina Proportione (1509), and to which leonardo contributes illustrations, continued to influence artists and mathematicians well into the fifteenth century.

26 The distinction is important. Piero emphasizes commensuratio as opposed to desegno. The rational character of his perspective process distinguishes it from Alberti. It is Bertelli who brings this distinction to light: ‘The constant norm that underlies the components of the representation and gives it geometric sense is called by Piero firstly commensuratio and only for greater clarity also perspectiva. Piero did not separate the mechanics of seeing from the representation of what is seen; the preceding quotations clearly demonstrate that he did not attribute arbitrary meanings to the representation of perspectives. … the choice of the term commensuratio, referring to geometric projection on a plane, which derives from the latinization of the greek symmetria (used by Vitruvius) as his own term is significant, particularly because in secondary instances he adopts prospectiva, a term that belongs to optics, as the more common synonym.’ C. Bertelli, ‘The science of seeing and of painting,’ Piero dell Francesca, Yale University Press, new haven, CT, 1992: 152.
